The impact of applying spectral transforms to the matrix pencil
s{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}
is examined.
In particular, two transforms are tested. The invert transform, which yields the dual
pencil
z{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}
; and the inverted Cayley transform,
i.e.
s=(z+1)/(\sigma z-\sigma)
, which yields the pencil

z({\boldsymbol{\rm E}}-\sigma{\boldsymbol{\rm A}})-(-\sigma{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm E}})
.
The results are shown in Table

2.14
. Passing the transformed matrices to
z-PARES provides a marginal speedup to the eigenvalue
computation. In addition, considering either the prime system or
the inverted Cayley transform with
\sigma=-1
, results in finding
the same number of eigenvalues, whereas when the dual system is
considered a number of eigenvalues is missed.

2.7
Concluding Remarks

This chapter provides fundamental concepts of power system
sssa
and linear systems of differential equations that are employed throughout the thesis.

The chapter also provides a comprehensive comparison of state-of-art software implementations for the numerical solution of the eigenvalue problems that arise in power systems. With this regard, the following discussion is relevant.

The main disadvantage of
dense matrix methods
is that they are computationally
expensive. In addition, they
generate complete fill-in in general sparse matrices and therefore, cannot be applied to large sparse matrices simply because of massive memory requirements. Even so, LAPACK is the most mature among all computer-based eigensolvers and, as opposed to basically all sparse solvers, requires practically no parameter tuning. For small to medium size problems, the QR algorithm with LAPACK remains the standard and most reliable algorithm for finding
the full spectrum for the conventional
lep
.

As for sparse matrix methods,
convergence of vector iteration methods can be very slow, and thus in practice, if
not completely avoided, these algorithms
should be used only for the solution of simple eigenvalue problems.
With regard to Krylov subspace methods, the main shortcoming of ARPACK’s implementation is the lack of support for general, non-symmetric left-hand side coefficient matrices, which is the form that commonly appears when dealing with the
gep
of large power system models. On the other hand, the implementations of

erd
-Arnoldi and Krylov-Schur by
SLEPc do not have this limitation and
exploit parallelism while providing good accuracy, although some parameter tuning effort is required.
In addition, for the scale of the
aiits
system and for the
gep
, these methods appear to be by far more efficient than LAPACK.
Moreover, the implementation of contour integration by z-PARES is very efficient and can handle systems at the scale of the
entsoe
. The most relevant issue for z-PARES is that, depending on the problem, it may miss some critical eigenvalues, despite the defined search contour being reasonable. Although there may be some parameter settings for
which this problem does not occur, those cannot be known
a priori
.

Chapter 3
Participation Factors

3.1
Introduction

Modal participation analysis was first introduced by Pérez-Arriaga

et al.
in
[
123
]
and
[
174
]
. These
studies employed the analytical solution that determines the time
response of a linear time-invariant dynamic system and applied initial
conditions appropriate to define the relative contribution of a system
state in a dynamic mode and
vice versa
.
pf
were introduced as an
approach to selective modal analysis. They have
been also utilized in model reduction
[
26
]
, as well as
in control signal and input placement selection
[
58
]
.
The
properties of
pf
were summarized and extended in

[
46
]
. In
[
3
,
54
]
, the authors
studied the effect of the uncertainty in the initial conditions in the
definition of the
pf
.
Nowadays,
pf
are
considered a fundamental tool for power system
sssa
.
Recent efforts have focused on the
modal participation analysis of non-linear systems
[
159
,
110
]
.

Dominant states in lightly damped modes of power systems are typically
the synchronous machine rotor angles and speeds. The state variables
of poorly tuned controllers, e.g. the
avr
and
pss
, can
also show high
pf
in critical modes. Nevertheless, measurement
units installed on the transmission system buses provide information
on the local voltage, frequency and active and reactive power flows,
which in angle and voltage stability studies are modeled as algebraic
variables
[
92
]
. Moreover, these quantities are
typically utilized by
facts
(
facts
) devices as
signals for the implementation of various controllers including

pod
(
pod
)
[
1
]
.

This chapter provides a tool to study how algebraic variables are coupled
with power system dynamic modes.
It is precisely recognized that the
algebraic variables of a set of
dae
can
be interpreted as functions of the state variables and, in turn, as
outputs
of the state-space representation
of the power system model. Until now,
algebraic variables were mostly interpreted either as

constraints
and thus eliminated when calculating the state
matrix of the system; or as
states with infinitely fast
dynamics
and, as such, their
pf
to system modes were
considered to be null.
The focus is on the
pf
of bus
voltages, frequencies, and power injections;

rocof
(
rocof
) of synchronous machines;
coi
(
coi
) speed of different areas; and any system
parameters. However, the formulation provided in this chapter is general
and can be extended to any non-linear function of the system states and algebraic variables.

The remainder of the chapter is organized as follows.
Section
3.2
describes the
classical
modal participation analysis of a power system model.
Section
3.3

provides
a new interpretation of the
pf
as eigen-sensitivities –
which is derived
from the partial differentiation of the analytical solution of the linearized power system around a valid equilibrium point –
and provides their formulation for a singular dynamical system with eigenvalue multiplicities.
Based on this interpretation, the proposed approach
to measure the participation of algebraic variables and,
in general, of any function of the variables in power system
modes is presented in Section
3.4
. The case studies are discussed in Section
3.5
. Finally, conclusions are
drawn in Section
3.6
.

3.2
Classical Participation Factors

3.2.1
Definition

Consider the following system
of
ode
:

\dot{\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}}={\boldsymbol{\rm A}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}\,,

(3.1)

where
{\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
is the vector of state variables and

{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
is the state matrix.
System (
3.1
) can be obtained from (
2.10
)
for
{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\equiv\boldsymbol{I}_{r}

and by assuming that no inputs are included.

Let
\lambda_{i}
be an eigenvalue of

s\boldsymbol{I}_{r}-{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}
and all eigenvalues be distinct, i.e.
\lambda_{i}\neq\lambda_{j}
,
i\neq j
, and

i,j=1,2,\ldots,r
.
Let also
\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{i}
,
\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{i}
be the right and left eigenvectors associated to
\lambda_{i}
, respectively.
The
pf

is defined as the following
dimensionless number:

{\uppi}_{k,i}=\mbox{$\text{w}$}_{i,k}\,\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,i}\,,

(3.2)

where
\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,i}
is the
k
-th row element of

\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{i}
and
\mbox{$\text{w}$}_{i,k}
is the
k
-th column
element of
\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{i}
.

The right and
left eigenvectors are usually normalized so that the sum of all
pf
that
correspond to the same eigenvalue equals to
1

[
123
]
. However, this is not always the case
[
75
]
. In addition,
the
pf
of a system are typically collected to form the participation matrix

\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF}
, which is defined as follows:

\mbox{$\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF}$}=\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\circ\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}\ ,

(3.3)

where
\circ
denotes the Hadamard product, i.e. the element-wise
multiplication; and
V
,
W
,
are the right and left
modal matrices
,
respectively.
That is, the columns of
V
are the right eigenvectors
\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{i}
and the rows
of
W
are the left eigenvectors

\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{i}
.

3.2.2
Residues

The
pf

{\uppi}_{k,i}
in
(
3.2
) basically expresses the relative contribution of
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}
in the structure of the eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}
, and
vice versa
, but has also
various other interpretations. It is also known to represent the sensitivity of an eigenvalue to
variations of an element of the state matrix
[
119
]
and it has been also viewed as modal energy in the MacFarlane sense

[
52
]
.

In the state space representation,

pf
can be studied as an important case of residue analysis of the system transfer function
and thus, as joint observabilities/controllabilities of the geometric
approach, which play an important role during the design of control
systems
[
53
,
46
]
.
Consider the following single-input single-output system:

\displaystyle\dot{\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}}

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\rm A}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}+\boldsymbol{b}\,\text{u}\,,

(3.4)

\displaystyle{w}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{c}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}\,,

where
\boldsymbol{b}
is the column vector of the input
u
;
\boldsymbol{c}
is
the row vector of the output
w
. Then, the residue of the
transfer function of system
(
3.4
) associated with the eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}

of the pencil

s\boldsymbol{I}_{r}-{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}
is given by:

\displaystyle\mbox{$\text{R}$}_{i}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{c}\,\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{i}\,\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{i}\,\boldsymbol{b}\,.

(3.5)

The
pf
of the
k
-th state
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}
in
\lambda_{i}
can be
viewed as the residue of the transfer function of system (
3.4
)
associated with
\lambda_{i}
, when the input is a perturbation in
the differential equation that defines
\dot{\mbox{$\text{x}$}}_{k}
and the
output is
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}
. Indeed, if

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{c}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}&\ldots&c_{k}&\ldots&c_{r}\\
\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\ldots&1&\ldots&0\\
\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{b}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}b_{1}&\ldots&b_{k}&\ldots&b_{r}\\
\end{bmatrix}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\ldots&1&\ldots&0\\
\end{bmatrix}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},

equation (
3.5
) becomes:

\displaystyle\mbox{$\text{R}$}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\text{w}$}_{i,k}\,\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,i}={\uppi}_{k,i}\ .

(3.6)

In the case of a multiple-input multiple-output system, the
pf
appear as the diagonal elements of the
emerging residue matrix. The ability to calculate only a subset of
all residue elements and acquire an approximate but yet accurate
measure of the contribution of system states in system modes (and

vice versa
), features the physical importance and the
computational efficiency of the
pf
.

3.3
Generalized Participation Factors

3.3.1
Formulation

From the definition of
pf
given in (
3.2
), it follows that the main assumptions of classical modal participation analysis are:

•

All eigenvalues are distinct.

•

The system is modeled as a set of
ode
, i.e. all eigenvalues are finite.

On the other hand, it is common
in the simulation of dynamic models that some eigenvalues are
repeated. For small size systems, it
may be possible to avoid multiplicities, e.g. by perturbation of some
parameters. But this is impractical for
real-world size systems.
Moreover, for large-scale systems, available algorithms
are typically able to find a partial solution
only of the
gep

and provided that the matrices are sparse, which implies that
the system is modeled as a set
of singular differential equations. Section
2.5
further elaborates on this point.

This section
presents an alternative interpretation of the
pf
as eigen-sensitivities.
In the view of addressing the issues mentioned above,
the focus is on the modal participation analysis of a singular system
of differential equations with eigenvalue multiplicities.
The proposed approach requires the
solution of the

gep
, as opposed to the
conventional
lep
, and thus fully exploits the sparsity of Jacobian matrices
[
100
]
. This allows utilizing solvers for eigenvalue
analysis that scale well and are suitable for large real-world
systems. Finally,
classical
pf
are extracted from the provided formulation as a special case.

Consider system (
2.10
) without any inputs, i.e.:

{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\dot{\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}}(t)={\boldsymbol{\rm A}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(t)\,,

(3.7)

where
{\boldsymbol{\rm E}},{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
,
\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(t):\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{r}
.
From Theorem
2.4.4.2
,
and by applying
{\boldsymbol{\rm B}}=\boldsymbol{0}_{r,p}
in (
2.19
), the
analytical solution of system (
3.7
) is:

\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(t)=\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\nu}\,e^{\boldsymbol{J}_{\nu}t}\,\boldsymbol{\rm c}\,.

(3.8)

In order to study the effects of eigenvalue multiplicities in
(
3.7
),
(
2.19
) has to be rewritten so that
the generalized eigenvectors
appear in the solution of the system explicitly
[
32
]
.

Firstly, let:

•

\alpha
be the number of Jordan blocks and
\hat{\lambda}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
,
i=1,2,...,\alpha
, be finite eigenvalue that corresponds to the
i
-th Jordan block and
\beta_{i}
be the rank of the block, where
\sum_{i=1}^{\alpha}\beta_{i}=\nu
.

•

the infinite eigenvalue have algebraic multiplicity
\mu
.

The following theorem is relevant.

\theorem

Consider system (
3.7
) with pencil
s{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}
and

{\rm det}(s{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm A}})\not\equiv 0
.
.
Let
\hat{\lambda}_{i}
,
i=1,2,...,\alpha
, be a finite eigenvalue of the pencil,
where
\alpha
is the number of Jordan blocks.
Let also

\beta_{i}
be rank of the corresponding Jordan block,
\sum_{i=1}^{\alpha}\beta_{i}=\nu
,
\alpha\leq\nu
, and
\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{i}^{[j]}
,
\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{i}^{[j]}
,
j=1,2,...,\beta_{i}
,
denote the
j
-th
right, left, linear independent (generalized) eigenvectors corresponding to the eigenvalue
\hat{\lambda}_{i}
, respectively.
Then
[
161
]
:

(a)

The solution of (
3.7
) with initial condition
\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)
can be written as follows:

\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{\alpha}e^{\hat{\lambda}_{i}t}\sum_{j=1}^{\beta_{i}}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{j}t^{k-1}\,\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{i}^{[j-k+1]}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)\Big)\,\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{i}^{[j]}\,.

(3.9)

(b)

Let
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(t)
be the
k
-th element of
\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(t)
. Then the participation of
\hat{\lambda}_{i}
in
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(t)
,
k=1,2,...,r
, is given by:

\frac{\partial\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(t)}{\partial e^{\hat{\lambda}_{i}t}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\beta_{i}}\Big(\sum_{\upsigma=1}^{j}t^{\upsigma-1}\,\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{i}^{[j-\upsigma+1]}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)\Big)\,\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,i}^{[j]}\,,\quad\text{(Participation Factor)}

(3.10)

where
\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,i}^{[j]}
is the
k
-th row element of the eigenvector
\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{i}^{[j]}
.

The proof of Theorem
3.3.1
is given in Appendix
A
.

The following corollary is relevant.

\corollary

Consider system (
3.7
) with a regular pencil.
Let the finite eigenvalues be either distinct, or with algebraic multiplicity equal to geometric, i.e.
\beta_{i}=1
is the rank of corresponding Jordan block.
Then in Theorem
3.3.1
, one has
\alpha=\nu
,
\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{i}^{[j]}=\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{i}
, and:

(a)

The solution of (
3.7
) with initial condition
\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)
is given by:

\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{\nu}\,\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{i}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)\,\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{i}\,e^{\lambda_{i}t}\,.

(b)

Let
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(t)
be the
k
-th element of
\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(t)
. Then the participation of the
i
-th eigenvalue,
i=1,2,...,\nu
, in
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(t)
,
k=1,2,...,r
, is given by:

\frac{\partial\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(t)}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}=\boldsymbol{\rm w}_{i}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)\,\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,i}\,,\quad\text{(Participation Factor)}

(3.11)

where
\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,i}
is the
k
-th row element of the eigenvector
\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{i}
.

The following remarks are relevant.

\remark

Since only the finite eigenvalues appear in (
3.9
), the
participation matrix of system (
3.7
) has dimensions

r\times\alpha
. Determining the
pf
associated with
the infinite eigenvalue to obtain the full matrix is possible by applying a
spectral transform to system (
3.7
).
In particular,
by applying
z=1/s
into (
3.7
), one arrives at the dual system

{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}\,\dot{\hat{}\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}}={\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\hat{}\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}
.
Let
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(t)
be the
k
-th element of
\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(t)
, and
\hat{\mbox{$\text{x}$}}_{k}(t)
be the
k
-th element of
\hat{\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}}(t)
. Then the participation of the infinite eigenvalue of
s{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}
in
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(t)
,
k=1,2,...,r
, is equal to the participation of the zero eigenvalue of
z{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}
in
\hat{\mbox{$\text{x}$}}_{k}(t)
,
k=1,2,...,r
.
This is a direct result from the duality between (
3.7
) and its dual system, or, additionally, between their pencils
s{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}
, and
z{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}
respectively, see
[
100
]
.
Note that this discussion, although interesting from a mathematical viewpoint, is not of practical
interest in power system
sssa
, since an
infinite eigenvalue does not represent any particularly meaningful dynamics.

\remark

Applying appropriate initial conditions in (
3.10
),
i.e.
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(0)=1
, and
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{h}(0)=0
,
h\neq k
, and imposing

t\rightarrow 0
,
allows obtaining the

pf
in the classical sense
[
123
]
.
Furthermore,
as already discussed,
algorithms that are suitable for large sparse matrices allow finding only a partial solution of
the
gep
, typically
including the most critical
dynamic modes of the system.
This solution
allows determining only the part
of the participation matrix that is associated with the most
critical modes. Therefore,
by applying the above initial conditions,
the (critical)
participation matrix, i.e. the part of the participation matrix that
is associated with the most critical eigenvalues of the system, can
be expressed as:

{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},\kappa}=\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\kappa}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\circ({\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\kappa})\ ,

(3.12)

where
\kappa
,
\kappa\leq\alpha
, be the number of the
calculated finite eigenvalues
and

\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\kappa}
,

\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\kappa}

are the corresponding right and left modal matrices.

3.3.2
Illustrative Example

As an illustrative example, consider system (
3.7
) with

{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}=\begin{bmatrix}12&-3&0&0&0\\
4&1&-1&3&0\\
0&-4&-5&1&0\\
8&2&-5&9&0\\
0&0&0&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}\,,\,{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}=\begin{bmatrix}-17&8&-2&5&3\\
-7&-3&3&-8&1\\
13&9&9&3&1\\
-12&-7&13&-22&0\\
1&0&0&0&1\\
\end{bmatrix}.

The pencil
s{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}
has
\alpha=2
finite eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{1}=-2
,
\hat{\lambda}_{2}=-3
, of algebraic multiplicity
\nu_{1}=2
,
\nu_{2}=1
and the infinite eigenvalue with multiplicity
\mu=2
. The geometric multiplicity
\gamma_{i}
of the finite eigenvalue
\hat{\lambda}_{i}
is found as the dimension of the null space of
\hat{\lambda}_{i}{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}
. In this example,
\gamma_{1}=1
,
\gamma_{2}=1
.
The right and left eigenvectors of
s{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}
associated with the finite eigenvalue
\hat{\lambda}_{1}=-2
are:

\footnotesize\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{1}^{[1]}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
-1\\
-1\\
0\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix},\ \mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{1}^{[2]}=\begin{bmatrix}0.0049\\
-3.282\cdot 10^{7}\\
-3.282\cdot 10^{7}\\
0\\
0.0049\\
\end{bmatrix},\ \mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{1}^{[1]}=\begin{bmatrix}-0.2308\\
-0.3846\\
0.0769\\
0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},\ \mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{1}^{[2]}=\begin{bmatrix}-0.1426\\
-0.2376\\
0.0475\\
0\\
0.6178\\
\end{bmatrix}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T},

where
\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{1}^{[2]}
,
\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{1}^{[2]}
are generalized eigenvectors determined from
({\boldsymbol{\rm A}}-\hat{\lambda}_{1}{\boldsymbol{\rm E}})\,\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{1}^{[2]}={\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{1}^{[1]}
and

\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{1}^{[2]}\,({\boldsymbol{\rm A}}-\hat{\lambda}_{1}{\boldsymbol{\rm E}})=\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{1}^{[1]}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}
respectively.
The right and left eigenvectors of
s{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}
associated with the finite eigenvalue
\hat{\lambda}_{2}=-3
are:

\mbox{$\textbf{v}$}_{2}^{[1]}=\footnotesize\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
-0.5\\
0\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix},\hskip 8.50012pt\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{2}^{[1]}=\begin{bmatrix}-0.3333\\
1\\
0.1111\\
0\\
-0.1111\\
\end{bmatrix}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}\,.

The sensitivities

\dfrac{\partial\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(t)}{\partial e^{\hat{\lambda}_{i}t}}
are obtained from Theorem
3.3.1
as follows:

\frac{\partial\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(t)}{\partial e^{\hat{\lambda}_{i}t}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\beta_{i}}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{j}t^{k-1}\,\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{i}^{[j-(k-1)]}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)\Big)\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,i}^{[j]}\,.

For
\hat{\lambda}_{1}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{2}
one has respectively:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(t)}{\partial e^{\hat{\lambda}_{1}t}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{j}t^{k-1}\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{1}^{[j-(k-1)]}{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)\Big)\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,1}^{[j]}

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{1}^{[1\,]}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)\,\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,1}^{[1]}+\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{2}t^{k-1}\,\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{1}^{[2-(k-1)]}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)\Big)\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,1}^{[2]}

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{1}^{[1]}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)\,\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,1}^{[1]}+\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{1}^{[2]}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)\,\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,1}^{[2]}+t\,\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{1}^{[1]}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)\,\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,1}^{[2]}\,,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(t)}{\partial e^{\hat{\lambda}_{2}t}}

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{2}^{[1]}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)\,\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,2}^{[1]}\,.

Consider
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{k}(0)=1
, and
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{i}(0)=0
,
i\neq k
, which lead to the
pf
related to the system finite modes. One has:

•

For
\dfrac{\partial\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{1}(t)}{\partial e^{\hat{\lambda}_{i}t}}
, one has

\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle{\uppi}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=0.0130+0.0209t\ ,\ {\uppi}_{1,2}=0\ .

•

For
\dfrac{\partial\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{2}(t)}{\partial e^{\hat{\lambda}_{i}t}}
, one has

\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0&0&0\end{bmatrix}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle{\uppi}_{2,1}

\displaystyle=0.3290+1.0839t\ ,\ {\uppi}_{2,2}=0.6667\ .

•

For
\dfrac{\partial\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{3}(t)}{\partial e^{\hat{\lambda}_{i}t}}
, one has

\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&1&0&0\end{bmatrix}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle{\uppi}_{3,1}

\displaystyle=0.6580+2.1678t\ ,\ {\uppi}_{3,2}=0.3333\ .

•

For
\dfrac{\partial\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{4}(t)}{\partial e^{\hat{\lambda}_{i}t}}
, one has

\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0&1&0\end{bmatrix}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle{\uppi}_{4,1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\ {\uppi}_{4,2}=0\,.

•

For
\dfrac{\partial\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{5}(t)}{\partial e^{\hat{\lambda}_{i}t}}
, one has

\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(0)=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0&0&1\end{bmatrix}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle{\uppi}_{5,1}

\displaystyle=0\ ,\ {\uppi}_{5,2}=0\ .

Results are summarized in
Table
3.1
, where
t\rightarrow 0
.
Since
{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\rm A}}
are
5\times 5
matrices and
{\rm rank}(s{\boldsymbol{\rm E}}-{\boldsymbol{\rm A}})=3
,
there exist
5-3=2
variables the participation of which to the system finite eigenvalues is zero. These
variables are
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{4}
and
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{5}
.
Moreover, Table
3.1
shows that
\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{3}
is dominant
in
\hat{\lambda}_{1}
, while

\mbox{$\text{x}$}_{2}
is dominant in
\hat{\lambda}_{2}
.

3.4
Participation Factors of Algebraic Variables

Consider the explicit
dae
power system model (
2.5
) without inputs, i.e.
{\boldsymbol{B}_{a}}=\boldsymbol{0}_{(n+m),p}
. Let
\mbox{$\text{z}$}_{k}(t)
be the
k
-th element of
\mbox{$\textbf{z}$}(t)
. Then, the following cases are relevant:

•

k\leq n
, that is
\mbox{$\text{z}$}_{k}
is a state variable.
Then, substitution of appropriate initial conditions
(see Remark
3.3.1
)
gives
\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}^{[1]}_{i}{\boldsymbol{E}_{a}}\mbox{$\textbf{z}$}(0)=\mbox{$\text{w}$}_{i,k}^{[1]}
.
In the special case that the eigenvectors form a complete basis for
the rational vector space of the matrix pencil, which means that all eigenvalues
are either distinct or their algebraic multiplicity is equal with the geometric, one has
\nu_{i}=1
,
\nu=n
, and thus

\mbox{$\text{v}$}^{[1]}_{k,i}=\mbox{$\text{v}$}_{k,i}
,
\mbox{$\text{w}$}^{[1]}_{i,k}=\mbox{$\text{w}$}_{i,k}
in
(
3.13
).
Substitution in (
3.10
) gives:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mbox{$\text{z}$}_{k}(t)}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}=\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}^{[1]}_{i}{\boldsymbol{E}_{a}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{z}$}(0)\,\mbox{$\text{v}$}^{[1]}_{k,i}=\mbox{$\text{w}$}^{[1]}_{i,k}\,\mbox{$\text{v}$}^{[1]}_{k,i}\ ={\uppi}_{k,i}\,.

(3.13)

•

n<k\leq n+m
, i.e.
\mbox{$\text{z}$}_{k}
is
an algebraic variable. Then

\mbox{$\textbf{w}$}_{i}^{[1]}\,{\boldsymbol{E}_{a}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{z}$}(0)=0
.
The
m
rightmost columns of
{\boldsymbol{E}_{a}}
which contain only zero
elements, impose that the
pf
of the algebraic variables in the
system finite modes are
found to be
null.
This is a consequence of the fact that
the coefficients of the first derivatives of the algebraic variables
are zero, which implies that the algebraic variables introduce only
infinite eigenvalues to the system.
Nevertheless, the algebraic variables
constrain the system and, in this sense,
do participate in the
system finite modes.

It follows that, for this system, the critical
participation matrix
(
3.12
) takes
the following form:

{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},\kappa}={\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\rm T}_{\kappa}}\circ({\boldsymbol{\rm E}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\kappa})=\begin{bmatrix}{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},\boldsymbol{x}}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{m,\kappa}\\
\end{bmatrix}\,,

(3.14)

where

{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},\mbox{$\text{x}$}}\subset\mbox{$\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}_{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF}$}

,

{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},\mbox{$\text{x}$}}\in\mathbb{C}^{(n+m)\times\kappa}
.
The matrix
{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},\mbox{$\text{x}$}}

contains all the information on the dynamics of interest and is the
matrix that is utilized in the remainder of the chapter.

This section introduces an approach to measure the participation
of algebraic variables in power system dynamic modes, based on the
pf
of the system states
[
161
]
. These can be algebraic
variables included in the
dae
system model, or, in general, any
algebraic output that is defined as a function of the states and
algebraic variables of the
dae
system.

Let the output vector
\boldsymbol{w}
,
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
be defined as:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{w}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{h}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})\,,

where

\boldsymbol{h}\,(\boldsymbol{h}:\mathbb{R}^{n+m}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q})
is
a non-linear function of
\boldsymbol{x}
,
\boldsymbol{y}
. Then differentiation
around
(\boldsymbol{x}_{o},\boldsymbol{y}_{o})
yields:

{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}={\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\Delta\boldsymbol{x}+{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}\,\Delta\boldsymbol{y}\,.

(3.15)

Substitution of (
2.8
) to the last equation gives:

\displaystyle{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{C}\,\Delta\boldsymbol{x}\,,

(3.16)

where
\boldsymbol{C}={\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}\,{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{-1}\,{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
,

\boldsymbol{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times n}
, is the output matrix.

Let
{\Delta w_{\varrho}}
be the
\varrho
-th system output. Then, the
following expression is a candidate as the
pf
of
{\Delta w_{\varrho}}
in the mode

\lambda_{i}
:

\displaystyle{\hat{\uppi}_{\varrho,i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\Delta w_{\varrho}}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}\ .

(3.17)

From the state-space viewpoint,
\hat{\uppi}_{\varrho,i}
expresses the residue
(or the joint observability/controllability)
of the
i
-th mode, when the input is,
exactly as it holds for
{\uppi}_{k,i}
, a perturbation in the differential
equation that defines
\Delta{\dot{x}}_{k}
.
The output however is

{\Delta w_{\varrho}}
, which can be, in principle, any function of the
system state variables. The fact that the perturbation that leads from
(
3.10
) and (
3.17
) to the classical
pf
is the same, is also the reason that

\hat{\uppi}_{\varrho,i}
is called
pf
.

\proposition

Let the
pf

{\hat{\uppi}_{\varrho,i}}
be the

\varrho
-th row,
i
-th column element of the participation matrix
{\hat{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},(\boldsymbol{w})}
. Then:

\displaystyle{\hat{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},(\boldsymbol{w})}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{C}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},\boldsymbol{x}}\,.

(3.18)

Proof.
Let
\boldsymbol{c}_{\varrho}=\begin{bmatrix}c_{\varrho,1}&\ldots&c_{\varrho,n}\\
\end{bmatrix}

be the
\varrho
-th row of
\boldsymbol{C}
.
Then:

\displaystyle\Delta w_{\varrho}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{c_{\varrho}}\,\Delta\boldsymbol{x}=c_{\varrho,1}\,\Delta x_{1}+c_{\varrho,2}\,\Delta x_{2}+\cdots+c_{\varrho,n}\,\Delta x_{n}\ .

Partial differentiation over
e^{\lambda_{i}t}
leads to:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Delta w_{\varrho}}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}

\displaystyle=c_{\varrho,1}\,\frac{\partial\Delta x_{1}}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}+c_{\varrho,2}\,\frac{\partial\Delta x_{2}}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}+\cdots+c_{\varrho,n}\,\frac{\partial\Delta x_{n}}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}+

\displaystyle+\frac{\partial c_{\varrho,1}}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}\,\Delta x_{1}+\frac{\partial c_{\varrho,2}}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}\,\Delta x_{2}+\cdots+\frac{\partial c_{\varrho,n}}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}\,\Delta x_{n}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\hat{\uppi}_{\varrho,i}

\displaystyle=c_{\varrho,1}\,{\uppi}_{1,i}+c_{\varrho,2}\,{\uppi}_{2,i}+\cdots+c_{\varrho,n}\,{\uppi}_{n,i}\ ,

where
\dfrac{\partial c_{\varrho,1}}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}=\dfrac{\partial c_{\varrho,2}}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}=\ldots=\dfrac{\partial c_{\varrho,n}}{\partial e^{\lambda_{i}t}}=0
, since the elements of
\boldsymbol{C}
do not depend on functions of
t
. By applying the same steps for
all outputs and representing in matrix form, one arrives at (
3.18
).

\blacksquare

The main feature of (
3.18
) is that it allows defining the
participation matrix not only of the algebraic variables of the

dae
, but also of any defined output vector that is a function of
the system state and algebraic variables. One has only to specify the
gradients
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
and
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}
at the operating point, and then
calculate the output matrix
\boldsymbol{C}
.
The proposed participation matrix
{\hat{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},(\boldsymbol{w})}
provides meaningful information
for the system coupling that, to the best of the authors’ knowledge,
has not been exploited in the literature.

\remark

The following special cases for the participation matrix of (
3.18
) are relevant:

(a)

State variables:
If
\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{x}
, the gradients in
(
3.15
) become
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{n}
,
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}=\boldsymbol{0}_{q,m}
.
The output matrix is
\boldsymbol{C}=\boldsymbol{I}_{n}
and hence the participation
matrix of the system states is, as expected:

\displaystyle{\hat{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},(\boldsymbol{x})}

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},\boldsymbol{x}}\,.

(3.19)

(b)

Algebraic variables:

If
\boldsymbol{w}=\boldsymbol{y}
, the
gradients in (
3.15
) become
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\boldsymbol{0}_{q,n}
,

{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{m}
. The output matrix is

\boldsymbol{C}=-{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{-1}\,{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
. Thus:

\displaystyle{\hat{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},(\boldsymbol{y})}

\displaystyle=-{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{-1}\,{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},\boldsymbol{x}}\,,

(3.20)

which is the participation matrix of the algebraic variables in
system modes included in the
dae
model.

(c)

Rates of change of state variables:
If the output is defined as
\boldsymbol{w}=\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}=\boldsymbol{f}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})
, the gradients in (
3.15
) become
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}={\boldsymbol{f}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
,

{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}={\boldsymbol{f}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}
. The output matrix is
\boldsymbol{C}=\boldsymbol{A}
. Thus:

\displaystyle{\hat{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},(\dot{\boldsymbol{x}})}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{A}\,{\boldsymbol{\rm\Pi}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle\rm PF},\boldsymbol{x}}\,.

(3.21)

The
rocof
of the
synchronous machines
(
\dot{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{\rm r}
) is a relevant
case.

(d)

Parameters:
Finally, consider the scalar output

w={\eta}
, where
{\eta}
is a parameter. If
{\eta}

appears only in the
j
-th algebraic equation

0=g^{j}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y},\eta)
, then
the linearization
of the
j
-th algebraic equation around the operating point yields:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{g}^{j}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\,\Delta{\boldsymbol{x}}+{\boldsymbol{g}^{j}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}\,\Delta{\boldsymbol{y}}+{g^{j}}_{\eta}\,\Delta{\eta}\ ,

(3.22)

where
{\boldsymbol{g}^{j}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times n}
,

{\boldsymbol{g}^{j}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times m}
and

{g^{j}}_{\eta}\in\mathbb{R}_{\neq 0}
. Solving (
3.22
) for

\Delta{\eta}
and comparing with (
3.15
),

{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=-{\boldsymbol{g}^{j}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\big/{g^{j}}_{\eta}
and

{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}=-{\boldsymbol{g}^{j}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}\big/{g^{j}}_{\eta}
. The participation vector is
obtained from (
3.18
) for

\boldsymbol{C}=(-{\boldsymbol{g}^{j}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}+{\boldsymbol{g}^{j}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}\,{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{-1}\,{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\boldsymbol{x}})\big/{g^{j}}_{\eta}
.

Note, finally, that once the eigenvalue analysis is completed and
the modal matrices are known, calculating the proposed participation
matrices involves few matrix multiplications. From the computational
burden viewpoint, the cost of calculating the
pf
is marginal compared
to the eigenvalue analysis.

3.5
Case Studies

This case study presents two practical applications of the
proposed approach and shows how defining
pf
of algebraic
variables in system modes can help design more effective and robust
controllers. In particular, Section
3.5.1
is based on the
well-known two-area system
[
74
]
and shows how the
calculation of
pf
can help select the most effective algebraic
variable to be measured to damp inter-area oscillations. Section

3.5.2
utilizes a realistic detailed model of the
aiits
and shows how
pf
can help define the impact
of a given system mode on the network. This second case study also
serves to discuss the robustness and the scalability of the proposed approach.
Simulations of this section are carried using Dome
[
93
]
.

3.5.1
Two-Area System

The two-area system is depicted in Figure
3.1
.
It comprises two areas connected through a relatively weak
tie; eleven buses and four synchronous machines.
Each generator is equipped with an
avr
of type IEEE DC-1 and a

tg
. The system feeds two loads connected to buses
7 and 9 and which are modeled as constant active
and reactive power consumption.

Figure 3.1
:
Two-area four-machine system: single-line diagram.

The system model has
52
state variables. For a system with this
dynamic order, the dense state matrix

{\boldsymbol{A}_{s}}
can be efficiently calculated and handled. The most critical modes and the mostly participating
states to these modes are presented in
Table
3.2
. Area 1
presents a critical local mode
-0.599\pm\jmath 6.604
with natural
frequency
1.06
Hz and dominant state the rotor speed
\omega_{{\rm r},2}
.
Area 2 presents a critical local mode as well, which is

-0.514\pm\jmath 6.843
with natural frequency
1.09
Hz and dominant
state the rotor speed
\omega_{{\rm r},4}
. For these modes, the damping
ratio is
>5\%
. Finally, the most lightly damped mode is

-0.096\pm\jmath 3.581
, which is an inter-area mode with natural
frequency
0.57
Hz. The mostly participating state in the inter-area
mode is the rotor speed
\omega_{{\rm r},3}
.

The participation matrix of the algebraic variables for these modes is
calculated from (
3.20
).
Note that in this section, each

{\hat{\uppi}_{\varrho,i}}
is divided over the Euclidean norm of the respective
output
\boldsymbol{c}_{\varrho}
, so that the results are normalized and
comparable according to the geometric approach. Of course, since the
pf
are a relative measure, one may apply any further normalization, e.g. the maximum or the sum of the values to be equal to 1.

The following simple test shows how the proposed
pf
of the
algebraic variables are linked to their sensitivities in eigenvalue
changes. Imposing a perturbation in the active power and voltage of
the PV buses 1, 4, changes the most critical mode

-0.096\pm\jmath 3.581
by
|d\lambda|=3\cdot 10^{-5}
. The
calculated eigen-sensitivities
{|d\Delta y_{k}|}\big/{|d\lambda|}
are then
compared with the
pf
of the algebraic variables
P_{1}
,

P_{4}
,
v_{1}
,
v_{4}
, in Table
3.3
. As
expected, a highly participating variable in a mode indicates that
this mode is sensitive to small variations of this variable.

.

For illustration, consider now the simple example of finding the
participation vector of one system parameter. Let
P_{7}
be the
active power consumption of the load connected to bus 7. Since

P_{7}
is also the active power injection at bus 7,
the following algebraic equation (see Figure
3.1
) holds:

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=v_{7}v_{6}(G_{76}\cos(\theta_{7}-\theta_{6})+B_{76}\sin(\theta_{7}-\theta_{6}))

\displaystyle+v_{7}v_{8}(G_{78}\cos(\theta_{7}-\theta_{8})+B_{78}\sin(\theta_{7}-\theta_{8}))-P_{7}

\displaystyle=g(v_{6},v_{7},v_{8},\theta_{6},\theta_{7},\theta_{8},P_{7})\ .

Linearization and solving for
\Delta P_{7}
yields:

\displaystyle\Delta P_{7}

\displaystyle=\big(\frac{\partial g}{\partial v_{6}}\Delta v_{6}+\frac{\partial g}{\partial v_{7}}\Delta v_{7}+\frac{\partial g}{\partial v_{8}}\Delta v_{8}+\frac{\partial g}{\partial\theta_{6}}\Delta\theta_{6}+\frac{\partial g}{\partial\theta_{7}}\Delta\theta_{7}+\frac{\partial g}{\partial\theta_{8}}\Delta\theta_{8}\big)\ \,,

where, the gradients are calculated at

(v_{6,o},v_{7,o},v_{8,o},\theta_{6,o},\theta_{7,o},\theta_{8,o})
;
and
{\partial g}\big/{\partial P_{7}}=-1
. Therefore,
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}=\boldsymbol{0}_{q,n}
, and
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}
is the
1\times m
row vector
which contains the gradients calculated above in the indices of

v_{6},v_{7},v_{8},\theta_{6},\theta_{7},\theta_{8}
; all other
elements of
{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}
are zero. The output matrix
\boldsymbol{C}
is

\boldsymbol{C}=-{\boldsymbol{h}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}\,{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\boldsymbol{y}}^{-1}\,{\boldsymbol{g}}_{\boldsymbol{x}}
,

\boldsymbol{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times n}
. The resulting participation
matrix is given by (
3.18
).

:

The active (
\boldsymbol{P}_{\rm B}
) and reactive (
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\rm B}
) power
injections on all system buses, as well as the
coi
speeds
(
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\rm CoI}
) of the two areas are defined as outputs and
their
pf
are obtained from (
3.18
). Correspondingly, the
system bus voltages (
\boldsymbol{v}_{\rm B}
), angles (
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\rm B}
)
and frequencies (
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\rm B}
) are included in the algebraic
variables of the
dae
. Thus, their
pf
are determined from
(
3.20
). With this aim, ideal frequency estimations of the
system buses are obtained by employing the frequency divider formula, proposed in

[
99
]
.
The formulation of the frequency divider in per units is as follows:

\textbf{B}_{\rm BB}\,\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\rm B}=-\textbf{B}_{\rm BG}\,\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\rm r}\,,

where
\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\rm B}
are the estimated bus frequency
deviations with respect to the reference synchronous speed;

\Delta\boldsymbol{\omega}_{\rm r}
are the synchronous machines rotor speed
deviations; and
\textbf{B}_{\rm BB}
,
\textbf{B}_{\rm BG}
are system
susceptance matrices that include the internal reactances of the
synchronous machines.
The accuracy, the numerical
robustness and the computational efficiency of the frequency divider have been discussed in
[
99
,
115
,
164
]
.

The
pf
of the
rocof
of the
synchronous machines (
\dot{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{\rm r}
) are determined
from (
3.21
).
The mostly participating of the above variables in the system critical
modes are summarized in Table
3.4
. It is worth observing that the bus
voltages, the reactive power injections and the
rocof
have a low
participation in the system critical modes. Mostly participating in
the inter-area mode is the bus frequency
\omega_{8}
. Similarly, the
bus frequency
\omega_{10}
is the one mostly participating in the
local mode of Area 2. Finally, the
coi
speed of Area 1
(
\omega_{{\rm CoI},1}
) is the one mostly participating in

-0.599\pm\jmath 6.604
, which is a local mode of this area.

Finally, the calculated
pf
can be utilized to
improve the dynamic behavior of the system. As already discussed,
the critical mode of the system is the inter-area mode and the mostly
participating variable (Table
3.4
) is the bus frequency

\omega_{8}
. A
svc
(
svc
) is installed at bus 8 with a
pod
loop

[
1
]
. The
pod
input signal is
\omega_{8}
. The
pod

output is considered as an additional input to the
svc
voltage
reference algebraic equation. The results are summarized in
Table
3.5
. The eigenvalue analysis shows that, after the
inclusion of the controller, the system is stable and all modes are properly damped.

3.5.2
All-Island Irish Transmission System

This section considers the real-world model of the
aiits
which has been also
discussed in Section
2.6.1
.
The dynamic order of the system is 1,480. Eigenvalue analysis
shows that the system is stable when subject to small disturbances.
The system presents both local machine modes and inter-machine modes.
Recall that, a local machine mode refers to a single machine
oscillating against the rest of the system. On the other hand, an
inter-machine mode refers to a group of machines of the same area
oscillating against each other
[
74
]
. The remainder of
this section shows two modes with different damping ratios and
natural frequencies. The examined modes are summarized in
Table
3.6
.

, with natural frequency

. The mode

sum to

.

. The mostly participating active power injection is the one of the bus

. Figure

shows that the lower

are several buses in a large area that have a high participation to
the inter-machine mode.

3.6
Conclusions

The chapter proposes a systematic analytical approach to quantify the
participation of the algebraic variables of a power system model, and
to general of any function of the system variables in the system
modes, through the definition of output vectors of the system’s
state-space formulation. The proposed approach, which describes an
alternative interpretation of the
pf
as eigen-sensitivities,
provides a high flexibility, since it allows determining
pf
of
states, algebraic variables, rates of change of system variables, as
well as of system parameters.

Regarding the computational burden of the
participation analysis, once the eigenvalue analysis is completed, the
cost of calculating the proposed
pf
is negligible.
Moreover,
the proposed approach allows exploiting the sparsity of
the
gep
matrix pencil and can lead to a significant speedup,
provided that a proper eigenvalue solver is employed.

Chapter 4
Fractional Order Control

4.1
Introduction

Fractional calculus is the analysis of non-integer order
differentials and integrals.
Although the first discussion on derivatives with
non-integer order dates back to Leibniz
[
81
]
, major
studies on fractional calculus started with Liouville
[
85
]
. The application of fractional calculus in control was introduced with the definition of the ideal cut-off
characteristic by Bode
[
19
]
and the first systematic study
of the frequency response of
foc
was done by Oustaloup

[
116
]
. In
[
125
,
126
]
, Podlubny
provided a comprehensive analysis of fractional
systems with applications to automatic control, and proposed the use of the
FOPID
(
FOPID
) controller.
The
FOPID
controller is
an extension of
the classical
pid
, and is characterized by five parameters:
three gains, namely proportional, integral, and derivative;
and two fractional orders, namely integral (
\alpha
) and derivative (
\beta
).
Employing a
FOPID
extends the four control points of the

pid
strategy to the plane defined by the fractional orders
\alpha
and
\beta

[
105
]
.
This is illustrated in Figure
4.1
.

foc

have been applied to various engineering fields, e.g. heat diffusion

[
62
]
and robotic time-delay systems

[
78
]
.
Recent efforts on
foc
have tackled several issues, such as
modeling and studying the impact of control limiters, see

[
121
]
, and
variable-order fractional orders, see
[
27
]
.
Finally, it is relevant to mention here that
fractional calculus
is a promising tool for applications not only in control systems, but in many other applications, for example physics
[
57
]
and biology
[
60
]
.

Figure 4.1
:
PID vs FOPID: from point to plane.

The potential of
foc
for power system applications has not been
discussed until very recently. Applications include automatic voltage
regulation of synchronous machines

[
183
,
156
,
120
]
; load frequency control

[
154
,
139
]
; damping control
[
24
]
; and
voltage control of distributed energy resource systems

[
36
]
.
These works mainly focus on the tuning of FOPID controllers through
heuristic algorithms, such as particle swarm
[
183
]
,
chaotic multi-objective
[
120
]
, and imperialist competitive
algorithm
[
154
]
. Analytical methods employ frequency
response criteria such as the desired gain crossover frequency

[
139
]
.

From a practical and simulation point of view, fractional dynamics are
typically approximated using appropriate rational order transfer
functions. Although various techniques have been proposed to define
such transfer functions
[
175
]
,
the most commonly utilized continuous method is the
ora

[
117
]
.
Therefore, the
ora
is the
method considered in this chapter.
The works cited above focus on applications and rely, for
the implementation of
foc
, on proprietary software tools which are
utilized as a
black-box
. This approach is indeed
fostered by the availability of several software tools for the design and simulation of
foc
, see, for example, the Matlab toolboxes
CRONE
[
118
]
, Ninteger
[
172
]
, and FOMCON

[
158
]
.

The main goal of this chapter is to provide a
systematic study of
foc
for power system applications.
The remainder of the chapter is organized as follows.
Section
4.2
outlines the theory of fractional calculus.
Section
4.3
discusses the stability of power systems with
inclusion of
foc
.
Section
4.4
focuses on the modeling,
computer implementation and tuning of
ora
-based
foc
.
Two case studies are presented in Sections
4.5.1
and

4.5.2
. Finally, conclusions are drawn in
Section
4.6
.

4.2
Essentials of Fractional Calculus

Fractional calculus is the
analysis of non-integer order differentials and integrals.
That is, it deals with the problem of extending the
differentiation and integration operators
\frac{d^{n}}{dt^{n}}
,

\int^{t}_{0}d^{n}(\tau)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, for real (or complex) number powers.
There exist several approaches
that address this problem.
A precise formulation
is given by the
RL
(
RL
) definition. Consider
a function
\phi:[0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. The idea
behind the
RL
definition is to first consider the
n
-fold
integration of
\phi(t)
and then extend
n\in\mathbb{N}
to any

\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. In its derivative form, the
RL
definition
reads
[
105
]
:

\phi^{(\gamma)}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\upmu-\gamma)}\frac{d^{\upmu}}{dt^{\upmu}}\Big(\int^{t}_{0}\frac{\phi(\tau)}{(t-\tau)^{\gamma-\upmu+1}}d\tau\Big)\,,

(4.1)

where
\gamma
,
\upmu-1<\gamma<\upmu
,
\upmu\in\mathbb{N}
, is the
fractional order; and

\phi^{(\gamma)}(t)={d^{\gamma}\phi}/{dt^{\gamma}}
.
The Laplace transform of (
4.1
) is:

\mathcal{L}\{\phi^{(\gamma)}(t)\}=s^{\gamma}\Phi(s)-\sum^{\upmu-1}_{j=0}s^{j}\phi^{(\gamma-j-1)}(0)\,,

(4.2)

where
s\in\mathbb{C}
and

\mathcal{L}\{{\phi}(t)\}=\Phi(s)
.
Equation (
4.2
) requires the knowledge of the fractional order
initial conditions
\phi^{(\gamma-j-1)}(0)
,
j=0,1,\ldots,\upmu-1
.
This raises an issue for engineering systems since, currently, only
integer order initial conditions are well understood and known for
physical variables. Other properties of the
RL
definition are also
counter-intuitive in the sense of classical differentiation. For
example, the
RL
derivative of a constant function is typically
unbounded at
t=0

[
126
]
.

With the aim of meeting the requirements of known physical variables
and systems, (
4.1
) was revisited by Caputo
[
23
]
.
Caputo’s definition of
\phi^{(\gamma)}(t)
reads:

\phi^{(\gamma)}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\upmu-\gamma)}\int^{t}_{0}\frac{\phi^{(\upmu)}(\tau)}{(t-\tau)^{\gamma-\upmu+1}}d\tau\,.

(4.3)

The Laplace transform of (
4.3
) is:

\mathcal{L}\{\phi^{(\gamma)}(t)\}=s^{\gamma}\Phi(s)-\sum^{\upmu-1}_{j=0}s^{\gamma-j-1}\phi^{(j)}(0)\,.

(4.4)

Equation (
4.4
) requires the knowledge of the initial
conditions
\phi^{(j)}(0)
,
j=0,1,\ldots,\upmu-1
, which in this case
are of integer order. This property is crucial for the solution of
initial value problems. In fact, for the purpose of
fractional control, that is of concern here, one needs to use a
definition with integer order initial conditions. This work utilizes the Caputo definition of fractional
derivative given in (
4.3
),
which is more consistent for control
applications and follows the properties of differentiation in the
classical sense. For example, the Caputo fractional
derivative of a constant function is zero.

In the special case that
0<\gamma<1
,
the Caputo definition (
4.3
) reduces to
[
20
]
:

\phi^{(\gamma)}(t)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-\gamma)}\int^{t}_{0}\frac{\dot{\phi}(\tau)}{(t-\tau)^{\gamma}}d\tau\,.

(4.5)

There are several other definitions of fractional
derivatives/integrals and choosing the appropriate one depends on the
application. For example, the Grünwald-Letnikov’s derivative is
relevant for the numerical solution of fractional differential
equations. It is important to emphasize that the theory of fractional
calculus applicable to the stability analysis and control of physical
dynamical systems is an active research topic and yet to be fully
understood. Recent efforts have addressed issues related to Caputo’s
formulation, for example its singular kernel for
t=\tau

[
23
,
12
]
.
Finally, note that fractional-order operators are not a straightforward generalization of the classical integer-order operators and, despite the several advances and
interesting recent studies, see e.g.
[
20
,
29
,
35
,
63
,
89
,
84
,
142
,
152
,
178
]
, the existing theory of fractional differential equations
is far from complete.

4.3
Power System with Fractional Order Control

This chapter focuses on the modeling and stability of power systems
with inclusion of
foc
.
Since a general theory of the stability of
non-linear fractional differential equations is not available, we
proceed as follows
[
162
]
. This section considers the conditions for the
stability of a linear (or linearized)
singular power system model with inclusion of a
foc
. These conditions help design the
foc
discussed in Section

4.4
. However, power system models are non-linear.
For this
reason, the design of
foc
is checked by solving numerical time domain
simulations of the fully-fledged non-linear model of the system and its
controllers in the case studies presented in
Section
4.5
.

4.3.1
Modeling

Consider the
dae
linearized power system model (
2.4
), which is repeated here for clarity:

{\boldsymbol{E}_{a}}\,\dot{\mbox{$\textbf{z}$}}={\boldsymbol{A}_{a}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{z}$}+{\boldsymbol{B}_{a}}\,\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\,,

(4.6)

where
l=n+m
and
{\boldsymbol{E}_{a}}
,
{\boldsymbol{A}_{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{l\times l}
,

{\boldsymbol{B}_{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{l\times p}
.

Let the vector of the system
output measurements
\boldsymbol{w}
,

\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
, be:

{\boldsymbol{w}}={\boldsymbol{C}_{a}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{z}$}+{\boldsymbol{D}_{a}}\,\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\,,

(4.7)

where
{\boldsymbol{C}_{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times l}
,

{\boldsymbol{D}_{a}}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times p}
.
Then, a multiple-input, multiple-output
foc

for the system
(
4.6
)-(
4.7
),
can be described by a set of fractional

dae
as follows:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{E}_{c,1}\,\dot{\boldsymbol{x}}_{c}+\boldsymbol{E}_{c,\gamma}\,{\boldsymbol{x}_{c}}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{A}_{c}\,{\boldsymbol{x}_{c}}+\boldsymbol{B}_{c}\,\boldsymbol{w}\ ,

(4.8)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{0}_{p,1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{C}_{c}\,{\boldsymbol{x}_{c}}+\boldsymbol{D}_{c}\,\boldsymbol{w}-\Delta{\boldsymbol{u}}\,,

where
\gamma
is the controller’s fractional order;
\boldsymbol{x}_{c}
,

\boldsymbol{x}_{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{\varsigma}
, is the vector of the
controller states;
\boldsymbol{E}_{c,1},\ \boldsymbol{E}_{c,\gamma},\ \boldsymbol{A}_{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{\varsigma\times\varsigma}
,

\boldsymbol{B}_{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{\varsigma\times q}
,

\boldsymbol{C}_{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times\varsigma}
,

\boldsymbol{D}_{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times q}
.
It is relevant to mention
that there are
foc
that introduce multiple, distinct fractional
orders.
Combining (
4.6
),
(
4.7
) and (
4.8
) yields the closed-loop system
representation. In matrix form:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{E}&\boldsymbol{0}_{l,\varsigma}&\boldsymbol{0}_{l,p}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{\varsigma,l}&\boldsymbol{E}_{c,1}&\boldsymbol{0}_{\varsigma,p}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{p,l}&\boldsymbol{0}_{p,\varsigma}&\boldsymbol{0}_{p,p}\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\dot{\mbox{$\textbf{z}$}}\\
\dot{\boldsymbol{x}_{c}}\\
\Delta\dot{\boldsymbol{u}}\\
\end{bmatrix}\hskip-4.2679pt+\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{0}_{l,l}&\boldsymbol{0}_{l,\varsigma}&\boldsymbol{0}_{l,p}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{\varsigma,l}&\boldsymbol{E}_{c,\gamma}&\boldsymbol{0}_{\varsigma,p}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{p,l}&\boldsymbol{0}_{p,\varsigma}&\boldsymbol{0}_{p,p}\\
\end{bmatrix}\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{z}$}^{(\gamma)}\\
\boldsymbol{x}_{c}^{(\gamma)}\\
\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{(\gamma)}\\
\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{A}&\boldsymbol{0}_{l,\varsigma}&\boldsymbol{B}\\
\boldsymbol{B}_{c}\boldsymbol{C}&\boldsymbol{A}_{c}&\boldsymbol{B}_{c}\boldsymbol{D}\\
\boldsymbol{D}_{c}\boldsymbol{C}&\boldsymbol{C}_{c}&\boldsymbol{D}_{c}\boldsymbol{D}-\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{z}$}\\
{\boldsymbol{x}_{c}}\\
\Delta{\boldsymbol{u}}\\
\end{bmatrix}\,,

or equivalently,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{M}\,\dot{\boldsymbol{\psi}}+\boldsymbol{M}_{\gamma}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm cl}\,{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\,,

(4.9)

where

\boldsymbol{M}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{E}&\boldsymbol{0}_{l,\varsigma}&\boldsymbol{0}_{l,p}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{\varsigma,l}&\boldsymbol{E}_{c,1}&\boldsymbol{0}_{\varsigma,p}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{p,l}&\boldsymbol{0}_{p,\varsigma}&\boldsymbol{0}_{p,p}\\
\end{bmatrix}\,,\,\boldsymbol{M}_{\gamma}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{0}_{l,l}&\boldsymbol{0}_{l,\varsigma}&\boldsymbol{0}_{l,p}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{\varsigma,l}&\boldsymbol{E}_{c,\gamma}&\boldsymbol{0}_{\varsigma,p}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{p,l}&\boldsymbol{0}_{p,\varsigma}&\boldsymbol{0}_{p,p}\\
\end{bmatrix}\,,

\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm cl}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{A}&\boldsymbol{0}_{l,\varsigma}&\boldsymbol{B}\\
\boldsymbol{B}_{c}\boldsymbol{C}&\boldsymbol{A}_{c}&\boldsymbol{B}_{c}\boldsymbol{D}\\
\boldsymbol{D}_{c}\boldsymbol{C}&\boldsymbol{C}_{c}&\boldsymbol{D}_{c}\boldsymbol{D}-\boldsymbol{I}_{p}\\
\end{bmatrix}\,,\,\boldsymbol{\psi}=\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{z}$}\\
{\boldsymbol{x}_{c}}\\
\Delta{\boldsymbol{u}}\\
\end{bmatrix}\,,

with

\boldsymbol{M},\ \boldsymbol{M}_{\gamma},\ \boldsymbol{A}_{\rm cl}\in\mathbb{R}^{\rho\times\rho}
, and
\rho=l+\varsigma+p
.

4.3.2
Stability

This section studies the stability of the closed-loop system (
4.9
), which is a singular system of differential equations having both first, and fractional order derivatives. With this aim,
the following property of the Caputo fractional derivative is relevant
[
84
]
:

\proposition

Let
\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)
,

\boldsymbol{\phi}(t)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}[0,T]^{n}
for some
T>0
, where
\mathcal{C}^{1}
denotes the set of continuously differentiable functions.
Then:

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{\phi}^{(a)}(t)]^{(b)}=[\boldsymbol{\phi}^{(b)}(t)]^{(a)}=\boldsymbol{\phi}^{(a+b)}(t)\ ,

(4.10)

where
a,b\in\mathbb{R^{+}}
, and
a+b\leq 1
.

Note that (
4.10
) does not
hold for the
RL
derivative.

Equation (
4.9
) can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{\,}\psi^{(\gamma+\beta)}+\boldsymbol{M}_{\gamma}\,\boldsymbol{\psi}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm cl}\,{\boldsymbol{\psi}}\,,

(4.11)

where
\gamma+\beta=1
.

Adopting the notation

\displaystyle\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\psi}\ ,\ \mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{2}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{(\gamma)}\ ,

one obtains

\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{1}^{(\gamma)}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{(\gamma)}=\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{2}
.
Making use of (
4.10
) yields

\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{2}^{(\beta)}=\boldsymbol{\psi}^{(\gamma+\beta)}
.
Substitution to (
4.11
) gives:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{M}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{2}^{(\beta)}+\boldsymbol{M}_{\gamma}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{2}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm cl}\,{\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{1}}\ \Rightarrow

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{M}\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{2}^{(\beta)}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm cl}{\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{1}}-\boldsymbol{M}_{\gamma}\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{2}\ .

(4.12)

Equivalently:

\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{I}_{\rho}&\boldsymbol{0}_{\rho,\rho}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{\rho,\rho}&\boldsymbol{M}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{1}^{(\gamma)}\\
\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{2}^{(\beta)}\\
\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{0}_{\rho,\rho}&\boldsymbol{I}_{\rho}\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm cl}&-\boldsymbol{M}_{\gamma}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{1}\\
\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{2}\\
\end{bmatrix}\,.

(4.13)

System (
4.13
) can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}\,{\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}}^{\Delta}

\displaystyle={\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}}\,{\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}}\,,

(4.14)

or, by recovering the time dependency, as:

{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}^{\Delta}(t)={\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}}\,\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(t)\,,

(4.15)

where

{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{I}_{\rho}&\boldsymbol{0}_{\rho,\rho}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{\rho,\rho}&\boldsymbol{M}\\
\end{bmatrix},\ {\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{0}_{\rho,\rho}&\boldsymbol{I}_{\rho}\\
\boldsymbol{A}_{\rm cl}&-\boldsymbol{M}_{\gamma}\\
\end{bmatrix},\ {\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{1}\\
\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{2}\\
\end{bmatrix},\ {\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}}^{\Delta}=\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{1}^{(\gamma)}\\
\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}_{2}^{(\beta)}\\
\end{bmatrix}.

We have
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}},{\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
,

\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}:[0,+\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{r}
, and

\beta,\gamma\in(0,1)
, where for simplicity the notation
r=2\rho
is used.

\theorem

Consider system (
4.15
). Then its
matrix pencil is given by
[
31
]
:

\displaystyle z(s)\,{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}-{\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}}\,,

(4.16)

where

z(s)=\begin{bmatrix}s^{\gamma}\boldsymbol{I}_{\rho}&\boldsymbol{0}_{\rho,\rho}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{\rho,\rho}&s^{\beta}\boldsymbol{I}_{\rho}\\
\end{bmatrix}\,.

The proof of Theorem
4.3.2
is given in Appendix
A
.

Assuming that the pencil (
4.16
)
is regular, or equivalently,

{\rm det}(z{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}-{\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}})\not\equiv 0
, it can be proven that there always exist solutions of
(
4.15
).
Then, similarly to the discussions of Chapter
2
, uniqueness of solutions depends on the given initial condition.
The interested reader may find a
a comprehensive theory for the existence and uniqueness of solutions
of systems in the form of (
4.15
) – with either regular or singular pencil –
in
[
31
]
.

The eigenvalues of the matrix pencil (
4.16
)
provide insight on the stability of system (
4.15
), or
equivalently, of system (
4.9
).
Since the pencil of system (
4.15
) is
a regular pencil,

s{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}-{\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}}
is also a regular pencil.
Hence and because of the structure of
{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}
there exist invariants of
the following type:

•

\kappa
finite eigenvalues of algebraic multiplicity
\nu_{i}
,

i=1,2,...,\kappa
;

•

an infinite eigenvalue of algebraic multiplicity
\mu
,

where
\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\nu_{i}=\nu
,
\nu+\mu=r
.

Let

\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}=\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\nu,\gamma}\\
\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{p,\beta}\\
\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{q,\gamma}\\
\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{q,\beta}\\
\end{bmatrix}\,,\ \mbox{$\textbf{V}$}=\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\nu,\gamma}&\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\nu,\beta}&\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{q,\gamma}&\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{q,\beta}\end{bmatrix}\,,

where
\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\nu,\gamma}\in\mathbb{C}^{\hat{\nu}\times r}
,
\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\nu,\beta}\in\mathbb{C}^{\bar{\nu}\times r}
,
\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\mu,\gamma}\in\mathbb{C}^{\tilde{\mu}\times r}
,
\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\mu,\beta}\in\mathbb{C}^{\bar{\mu}\times r}
, and
\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\nu,\gamma}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times\hat{\nu}}
,
\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\nu,\beta}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times\bar{\nu}}
,
\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\mu,\gamma}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times\hat{\mu}}
,
\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\mu,\beta}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times\bar{\mu}}
. Equivalently, setting:

\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\nu}=\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\nu,\gamma}\\
\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\nu,\beta}\end{bmatrix}\,,\ \mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\nu}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}}_{\nu,\gamma}&\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\nu,\beta}\end{bmatrix}\,,

\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\mu}=\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\mu,\gamma}\\
\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\mu,\beta}\end{bmatrix}\,,\ \mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\mu}=\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\mu,\gamma}&\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\mu,\beta}\end{bmatrix}\,,

one has

\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}=\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\nu}\\
\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\mu}\end{bmatrix}\,,\ \mbox{$\textbf{V}$}=\begin{bmatrix}\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\nu}&\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\mu}\end{bmatrix}\,.

(4.17)

with
\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu\times r}
,
\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}_{\mu}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mu\times r}
, and
\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times\nu}
,
\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\mu}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times\mu}
.

Employing (
2.18
) for

{\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}},{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}
, and using the notation

\boldsymbol{I}_{\nu}=\boldsymbol{I}_{\hat{\nu}}\oplus\boldsymbol{I}_{\bar{\nu}}
,
\boldsymbol{J}_{p}=\boldsymbol{J}_{\hat{\nu}}\oplus\boldsymbol{J}_{\bar{\nu}}
, one has:

\displaystyle\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{I}_{\hat{\nu}}\oplus\boldsymbol{I}_{\bar{\nu}}\oplus\boldsymbol{H}_{\hat{\mu}}\oplus\boldsymbol{H}_{\bar{\mu}}\,,

\displaystyle\mbox{$\textbf{W}$}{\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}}\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{J}_{\hat{\nu}}\oplus\boldsymbol{J}_{\bar{\nu}}\oplus\boldsymbol{I}_{\hat{\mu}}\oplus\boldsymbol{I}_{\bar{\mu}}\,.

Finally, the following proposition is relevant:

\proposition

Consider system (
4.15
) with a regular pencil. Then:

a)

using the spectrum of the pencil
s{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}-{\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}}
, the general solution is given by:

\mbox{$\textbf{x}$}(t)=\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\nu}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{t^{\gamma k}}{\Gamma(k\gamma+1)}\boldsymbol{I}_{\hat{\nu}}&\boldsymbol{0}_{\hat{\nu},\hat{\nu}}\\
\boldsymbol{0}_{\bar{\nu},\bar{\nu}}&\frac{t^{\beta k}}{\Gamma(k\beta+1)}\boldsymbol{I}_{\bar{\nu}}\end{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{J}_{\nu}^{k}\,\textbf{c}\,,

(4.18)

where
\boldsymbol{J}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu\times\nu}
is a Jordan matrix constructed by the finite eigenvalues of the pencil
s{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}-{\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}}
, and their algebraic multiplicity, while
\mbox{$\textbf{V}$}_{\nu}\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times\nu}
is a matrix constructed by the linear independent eigenvectors related to the finite eigenvalues of the pencil
s{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}-{\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}}
, and

\boldsymbol{\rm c}\in\mathbb{C}^{\nu}

is a constant vector.

b)

the system (
4.15
) is asymptotically stable if all eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}^{*}
of the pencil
s{\tilde{\boldsymbol{E}}}-{\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}}
satisfy:

|\rm Arg(\hat{\lambda}^{*})|>\tilde{\gamma}\,\frac{\pi}{2}\ (rad)\,,

(4.19)

where
\tilde{\gamma}=\rm min\left\{\gamma,1-\gamma\right\}
.

The proof of Proposition
4.3.2
can be found in
[
31
]
. For a more general survey on the stability conditions for systems of fractional differential equations, the interested reader may refer to
[
124
]
.
Finally,
for linearized systems, as it is the case of power
systems, the condition (
4.19
) guarantees stability in a
neighborhood of the operating point utilized to calculate the pencil
(
4.16
). For this reason, in the case studies
discussed in Sections
4.5.1
and
4.5.2
,
numerical integration rather than
(
4.19
) is utilized to check the stability and the dynamic response
of power systems with inclusion of
foc
.

4.3.3
Properties of Fractional Order Controllers

Consider the simple
foc
with transfer function

H_{\rm c}(s)=K{s^{\gamma}}
.
Consider the
frequency response of

H_{\rm c}(s)
, i.e.
its steady-state response to sinusoidal, periodic input
signals. In this case, it is
s\in\mathbb{I}
,
or
s=\jmath\omega
.

Frequency Response:

The magnitude and phase of
H_{c}(s)
can be written as follows:

\displaystyle{\rm Mag}(H_{c}(s))\ {\rm(dB)}

\displaystyle=20\,{\rm log}|Ks^{\gamma}|=20\,\gamma\,{\rm log}(K\omega)\ ,

(4.20)

\displaystyle{\rm Arg(H_{c}(s))}\ {\rm(^{\circ})}

\displaystyle={{\rm Arg}(K(\jmath\omega)^{\gamma})}=90\,\gamma\,.

Hence,
H_{c}(s)
has a magnitude Bode plot with constant slope of

20\gamma
dB/dec, while the phase plot is a horizontal line at

90\gamma
degrees. The
IO
(
IO
) versions of
H_{c}(s)
are obtained for

\gamma=n
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, from (
4.20
), it is
clear that
H_{c}(s)

is an extension of its
IO
versions in frequency domain. This result
is general, so that all
foc
can be viewed as extensions of the
respective
IO
ones.

Robustness:

foc
have an inherent property of iso-damping, which
implies that the closed-loop system is robust against gain
uncertainties and variations. Let
H_{o}(s)
be the transfer function of
the open-loop, linearized power system. Then, the iso-damping
property is defined as:

\displaystyle\Bigg|\frac{d\;{\rm Arg}(H_{c}(\jmath\omega)\,H_{o}(\jmath\omega))}{d\omega}\Bigg|_{\omega=\omega_{\rm gc}}

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(4.21)

where
\omega_{\rm gc}
is the system gain crossover frequency. (
4.21
) indicates that the system maintains its phase margin
around
\omega_{\rm gc}
.

4.3.4
Examples

This section provides two illustrative examples
on the
sssa
of power systems with inclusion of
foc
. The first example considers a small linear singular system of differential equations with regular pencil. The second example discusses the damping of the electro-mechanical oscillations of the
wscc
9-bus system
through a
fopss
(
fopss
).

(4.22)

\displaystyle w
